An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\nThe larger absolute gap at 80 relations does not reflect stronger per-unit sensitivity; the within-pool analysis below shows slopes are indistinguishable across pools ($p = 0.60$). The 80-relation pool simply spans a wider BCS range, enabling more extreme batches.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across independently sampled subsets (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; full methodology in Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\\,pp) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the 31.2\\,pp extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt (stricter format constraints), the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.0001$). Compared to v1's $-$31.2\\,pp, the effect attenuates modestly ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant. Part of this attenuation reflects the narrower BCS range available under our v2 balanced sampling (0.36--0.80 vs.\\ 0.47--0.97); however, the stronger per-pair correlation ($r = -0.919$) under v2 suggests that per-unit sensitivity to confusability is at least as large when frequency and sample-size confounds are controlled. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates the magnitude but does not eliminate the confusability effect (full analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows extraction quality degrades monotonically with schema size across three datasets: Few-NERD recall drops from 0.72 to 0.42 ($-$42\\%), FewRel accuracy from 85.7\\% to 50.4\\% ($-$35.3\\,pp), and DocRED from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% ($-$56\\%). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty.\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt (stricter format constraints), the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.0001$). Compared to v1's $-$31.2\\,pp, the effect attenuates modestly ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant. Part of this attenuation reflects the narrower BCS range available under our v2 balanced sampling (0.36--0.80 vs.\\ 0.47--0.97); however, the stronger per-pair correlation ($r = -0.919$) under v2 suggests that per-unit sensitivity to confusability is at least as large when frequency and sample-size confounds are controlled. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates the magnitude but does not eliminate the confusability effect (full analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "8dc58577-48a0-4649-b1ff-1cb9e70b10a2",
  "uuid": "68d1e01b-a374-43cd-899f-64c3e366ac55",
  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex",
    "oldString": "$p < 10^{-9}$",
    "newString": "$p < 0.001$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 10^{-9}$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p = 2.56 \\times 10^{-26}$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the text string matches (case-insensitive) a gold entity \\emph{and} the type matches. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).     "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p = 2.56 \\times 10^{-26}$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the text string matches (case-insensitive) a gold entity \\emph{and} the type matches. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\n\\usepackage{listings}\n\n\\title{Does Type Confusability Shape Schema-Induced Degradation\\\\in Training-Free LLM Information Extraction?}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\input{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\n\\input{related_work}\n\n\\input{experimental_setup}\n\n\\input{results}\n\n\\input{sdgd_negative}\n\n\\input{discussion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Channel~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Non-significant factorial test.} Within-dataset count $\\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}); cross-pool slope consistency suggests confusability sensitivity is stable rather than amplifying.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions.\n\n\\item \\textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean channel separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\\%$\\to$50.4\\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\input{checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nWe conducted a within-dataset $2\\!\\times\\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p = 0.598$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & 0.011 & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & 0.107 \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.226 \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & 0.025 & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & 0.442 \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.439 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$31.2\\,pp (low-BCS 82.3\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 51.0\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}). Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.0001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.0001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}10^{-12}$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p = 0.598$; MAX $p = 0.436$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\\,pp) reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & 0.762 & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p = 0.598$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p = 0.598$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p=0.762$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 10^{-10}$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nWe verify that the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{enumitem}\n\\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}\n\\usepackage{listings}\n\n\\title{Does Type Confusability Shape Schema-Induced Degradation\\\\in Training-Free LLM Information Extraction?}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\input{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\n\\input{related_work}\n\n\\input{experimental_setup}\n\n\\input{results}\n\n\\input{sdgd_negative}\n\n\\input{discussion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Channel~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Non-significant factorial test.} Within-dataset count $\\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}); cross-pool slope consistency suggests confusability sensitivity is stable rather than amplifying.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions.\n\n\\item \\textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean channel separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\\%$\\to$50.4\\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\input{checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\n\\section{Additional Figures}\n\\label{sec:appendix_figures}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 0.702 & 0.640 \\\\\n64 & 0.273 & 0.304 \\\\\n\\midrule\nDecline & $-$43\\,pp & $-$34\\,pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count-curve replication on Few-NERD (text-match recall). Both models show a general declining trend in recall with schema size. Per-batch correlation $r = 0.725$. Qwen3-32B values use the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}) for fair cross-model comparison; the count-curve results in \\S\\ref{sec:count_curves} use the v1 prompt, yielding higher absolute recall (0.72 vs.\\ 0.64 at count${}\\,=\\,1$) due to over-prediction under v1.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{crrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Llama-3.1-8B}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Qwen3-32B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n8 & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n16 & 75.5\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$23.9 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy.  High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% regardless of model, while low-BCS accuracy reflects model capacity differences.  The confusability gap ($\\Delta$, in pp) is 77--87\\% as large on Llama-3.1-8B as on Qwen3-32B.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n & & \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{BCS Bin}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-7}\n\\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{Error} & \\textbf{Solo} & \\textbf{Low} & \\textbf{Mid} & \\textbf{High} & \\textbf{V.High} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & IntraSub & 0\\% & 1.6\\% & 10.6\\% & 49.4\\% & --- & 1.000 & ${<}0.001$ \\\\\nRE  & IntraSub & 0\\% & 4.2\\% & 13.0\\% & 20.3\\% & 32.3\\% & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNER & Missed & --- & 42.8\\% & 64.8\\% & 49.9\\% & --- & --- & --- \\\\\nRE  & Missed & --- & 10.4\\% & 11.3\\% & 13.1\\% & 15.7\\% & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) and missed-entity rates by BCS bin, with Spearman $\\rho$ computed over $n=154$ individual batches (RE) and $n=148$ batches (NER). NER has 4 BCS bins; RE has 5.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent---i.e., highly confusable---types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union. We compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nWe conducted a within-dataset $2\\!\\times\\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p = 0.598$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & 0.011 & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & 0.107 \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.226 \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & 0.025 & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & 0.442 \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & 0.439 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$31.2\\,pp (low-BCS 82.3\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 51.0\\%; Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}). Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.0001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\\,=\\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.0001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}10^{-12}$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p = 0.598$; MAX $p = 0.436$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\\,pp) reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Within-Pool Analysis Methodology}\n\\label{app:within-pool-method}\n\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & 0.762 & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p = 0.598$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p = 0.598$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}).\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p=0.762$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 10^{-10}$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nWe verify that the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\end{document}\n",
